========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure. I mean, I know that sounds like a simple yes-or-no question, but I have a hard time separating “not enjoying things” from just being tired or distracted or going through the motions.

There are things I still do, and sometimes I can enjoy them a little once I’m already doing them, but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve clearly lost interest in everything. It’s more like I don’t always feel that same pull toward things, if that makes sense. Like I might think, “Yeah, I should do that,” but then it feels like effort to start, and by the time I do, I’m already kind of checked out.

But I don’t know if that counts as not enjoying things as much. I haven’t really sat down and compared it to how I used to be. I wouldn’t want to say yes too strongly if I’m not sure.

I don’t know if I can say that clearly, honestly. I keep trying to think of examples, like things I used to look forward to, and I can’t tell if the pleasure is actually less or if I’m just not paying attention to it the same way.

Like, sometimes I’ll do something and afterward I don’t feel that satisfied, but then I also wonder if I was expecting too much from it, or if I was already kind of in my head before it even started. It’s not like I can point to one thing and say, “I used to love this and now I feel absolutely nothing.” It’s more blurry than that.

So I guess over the past few months, maybe there have been moments where things felt kind of muted, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as definitely losing pleasure or satisfaction. I haven’t really been thinking about it in those terms until you asked.

It’s not like I can point to one thing and say, “I used to love this and now I feel absolutely nothing.” It’s more blurry than that.\n\nSo I guess over the past few months, maybe there have been moments where things felt kind of muted, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as definitely losing pleasure or satisfaction.     "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Usually it’s already pretty low, honestly. Like before anything has even happened, before I’ve checked my phone or talked to anyone or gotten ready for work, I’ll wake up and there’s already this heavy, gray kind of feeling there.

It’s not always like I’m thinking one specific thing right away. It’s more like the mood is already in the room before I am, if that makes sense. I’ll just be lying there and it feels like the day is already hard, even before I’ve started it. And then when I’m getting ready to go to the clinic, that feeling kind of comes with me. It doesn’t really lift just because I’m moving around or doing what I’m supposed to do.

That’s actually a big part of why I came in. It’s been going on for a long time, where the morning starts low and then it pretty much follows me through the day.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d say it’s been exactly the same every single day, like no variation at all, because I don’t want to make it sound more precise than I can really track. But the basic feeling, yeah, it’s been there for a long time.

That’s really why I came in. I’ll wake up and before anything has even happened, before I’ve gotten to the clinic or talked to anyone, I already feel low. It’s like this heavy gray feeling is just sitting on me. And then I get up and do what I’m supposed to do, but it doesn’t really leave. It follows me through the day, even when there isn’t some obvious thing causing it.

So maybe there are small shifts depending on the day, but overall, it feels pretty constant. Like my default mood is low, and it’s been that way long enough that I don’t really remember what a normal morning is supposed to feel like.

I’ll go through the day doing what I’m supposed to do, talking to people, trying to keep up with work, but it’s like that feeling is just following me around the whole time. It’s been going on long enough that I finally thought I probably shouldn’t keep pretending it’s normal.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way during this current episode?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about two years and two months, I think. I kind of mark it from when the clinic staffing changed, because that’s when everything started getting shuffled around and my schedule started rotating all over the place. Before that I had stressful days, obviously, but after that change it just felt like this low feeling settled in and never really went away. So yeah, a little over two years now.\n\nDoctor: Have there been any times during these two years when you felt completely back to your usual self, even for a few weeks or months?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I’ve had days where I could get through things a little better, or I’d be distracted enough that it wasn’t right in my face every second, but I don’t think there’s been a stretch where I felt completely like myself again.\n\nIt’s been pretty continuous since that staffing change at the clinic, when my schedule started rotating all over the place. That’s kind of the point I keep coming back to, because before that I had normal stressful periods, but after that it just became this… ongoing thing. So for about two years and two months, I haven’t had a few weeks or months where I’d say, “Yeah, I’m back to normal.”\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep recently, like trouble falling asleep, staying asleep, or sleeping more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I haven’t really been tracking my sleep in a clear way, like writing it down or anything. Some nights feel worse than others, but I don’t know if I can honestly say there’s been a definite pattern recently, like consistently not falling asleep or waking up all night or sleeping way more.\n\nI guess I notice it more as just… I don’t wake up feeling refreshed, but I’m not sure if that’s because of my sleep itself or just because of how I’ve been feeling overall. So, yeah, I don’t know that I’ve noticed a specific sleep change recently.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, or that you’ve lost interest in activities you once found pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I know that sounds like a simple yes-or-no question, but I have a hard time separating “not enjoying things” from just being tired or distracted or going through the motions.\n\nThere are things I still do, and sometimes I can enjoy them a little once I’m already doing them, but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve clearly lost interest in everything. It’s more like I don’t always feel that same pull toward things, if that makes sense. Like I might think, “Yeah, I should do that,” but then it feels like effort to start, and by the time I do, I’m already kind of checked out.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as not enjoying things as much. I haven’t really sat down and compared it to how I used to be. I wouldn’t want to say yes too strongly if I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the past few months, have you found yourself feeling like you don’t get the same sense of pleasure or satisfaction from things, even if you used to look forward to them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that clearly, honestly. I keep trying to think of examples, like things I used to look forward to, and I can’t tell if the pleasure is actually less or if I’m just not paying attention to it the same way.\n\nLike, sometimes I’ll do something and afterward I don’t feel that satisfied, but then I also wonder if I was expecting too much from it, or if I was already kind of in my head before it even started. It’s not like I can point to one thing and say, “I used to love this and now I feel absolutely nothing.” It’s more blurry than that.\n\nSo I guess over the past few months, maybe there have been moments where things felt kind of muted, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as definitely losing pleasure or satisfaction. I haven’t really been thinking about it in those terms until you asked.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your mood typically feels first thing in the morning, before you’ve started your day or interacted with others?\n\nPatient: Usually it’s already pretty low, honestly. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think I’ve noticed anything major like that. Not in a way where I could say, “Yeah, I lost a lot of weight,” or “I’ve been eating way more than usual.” My appetite kind of depends on the day, I guess, but I haven’t been tracking it closely.

Sometimes I don’t feel that interested in food, but I still eat because it’s just part of the routine, or because I know I should. Other times I’ll eat normally and not really think about it. I haven’t had people telling me I look different or anything, and my clothes don’t fit noticeably differently, at least not that I’ve paid attention to.

So, no, I wouldn’t say there’s been a significant weight change or a big appetite change that stands out to me. It’s more that I probably haven’t been paying enough attention to be totally exact.

So, yeah, I don’t know that I’ve noticed a specific sleep change recently.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, or that you’ve lost interest in activities you once found pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. Like I might think, “Yeah, I should do that,” but then it feels like effort to start, and by the time I do, I’m already kind of checked out.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as not enjoying things as much. I’ll wake up and before anything has even happened, before I’ve gotten to the clinic or talked to anyone, I already feel low. It’s like this heavy gray feeling is just sitting on me. And then I get up and do what I’m supposed to do, but it doesn’t really leave. It follows me through the day, even when there isn’t some obvious thing causing it.\n\nSo maybe there are small shifts depending on the day, but overall, it feels pretty constant. Like my default mood is low, and it’s been that way long enough that I don’t really remember what a normal morning is supposed to feel like.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of years, have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight — like eating much less or more than usual, or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed anything major like that. Not in a way where I could say, “Yeah, I lost a lot of weight,” or “I’ve been eating way more than usual.” My appetite kind of depends on the day, I guess, but I haven’t been tracking it closely.\n\nSometimes I don’t feel that interested in food, but I still eat because it’s just part of the routine, or because I know I should. Other times I’ll eat normally and not really think about it. I haven’t had people telling me I look different or anything, and my clothes don’t fit noticeably differently, at least not that I’ve paid attention to.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say there’s been a significant weight change or a big appetite change that stands out to me. It’s more that I probably haven’t been paying enough attention to be totally exact.\n\n[Last Question]\nOver the past couple of years, have you found yourself feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, or feeling like you’re not worth much?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 20 / medical_assistant / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I don’t walk around thinking, like, “I’m worthless” in those exact words all day or anything. And I don’t know if I’d say guilt is the main thing I notice.

I do second-guess myself a lot, though, and I can be hard on myself over small things. Like if I mess something up or feel like I should’ve handled something better, I’ll replay it more than I probably need to. But I don’t know if that’s more than usual or just how I’ve always been. It’s kind of hard to separate.

As far as blaming myself for things, I guess sometimes I do, but I’m not sure it’s this huge, obvious change over the last couple years. It’s more like… if I’m already feeling low, then my mind can kind of go in that direction, like I should be doing better or I’m not where I’m supposed to be. But I don’t know if I’d describe it as constant guilt or feeling completely worthless.

So, I guess the honest answer is: I’ve noticed some self-critical thoughts, but I’m not sure how much of it counts as guilt or worthlessness in the way you mean.

I haven’t had people telling me I look different or anything, and my clothes don’t fit noticeably differently, at least not that I’ve paid attention to.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say there’s been a significant weight change or a big appetite change that stands out to me. It’s more that I probably haven’t been paying enough attention to be totally exact.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of years, have you found yourself feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, or feeling like you’re not worth much?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I don’t walk around thinking, like, “I’m worthless” in those exact words all day or anything. And I don’t know if I’d say guilt is the main thing I notice.\n\nI do second-guess myself a lot, though, and I can be hard on myself over small things. Like if I mess something up or feel like I should’ve handled something better, I’ll replay it more than I probably need to. But I don’t know if that’s more than usual or just how I’ve always been. It’s kind of hard to separate.\n\nAs far as blaming myself for things, I guess sometimes I do, but I’m not sure it’s this huge, obvious change over the last couple years. It’s more like… if I’m already feeling low, then my mind can kind of go in that direction, like I should be doing better or I’m not where I’m supposed to be. But I don’t know if I’d describe it as constant guilt or feeling completely worthless.\n\nSo, I guess the honest answer is: I’ve noticed some self-critical thoughts, but I’m not sure how much of it counts as guilt or worthlessness in the way you mean.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed that you've been moving more slowly than usual or, on the contrary, have been restless or fidgety more days than not?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed something obvious like people telling me I’m moving really slowly, or that I can’t sit still or anything like that.

At work, I still kind of do the things I have to do, so I’m walking around, rooming patients, getting supplies, whatever needs to be done. I don’t think anyone has said I seem physically slowed down. But at the same time, there are definitely moments where everything feels like it takes more effort, so I might be slower getting started with things. I don’t know if that counts as moving slower, though, because from the outside I might look pretty normal.

And with restlessness, I guess I fidget sometimes, like tapping my foot or shifting around, but I’ve never really thought of it as a big symptom or something that’s happening more days than not. It’s hard for me to tell because I’m not really watching myself that way.

So I guess my answer would be: I haven’t clearly noticed either one in a major way. Maybe some sluggishness or fidgeting here and there, but nothing that stands out enough for me to say yes confidently.

So, yeah, I don’t know that I’ve noticed a specific sleep change recently.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, or that you’ve lost interest in activities you once found pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d say “couldn’t enjoy” exactly. That sounds kind of strong, like I’m totally unable to feel anything from it, and I’m not sure that’s been the case.

There have been times where things I normally like just felt… less interesting, I guess. Like I might still do them, but it feels more like I’m going through the motions than actually getting into it. But then sometimes I can still enjoy parts of things, especially if I’m already there or someone else kind of gets me involved. So it’s hard for me to separate whether I’m not enjoying it, or whether I’m just tired of the routine, or whether I’m overthinking it.

I wouldn’t say there’s one clear thing where I used to love it and now I can’t stand it or get nothing from it. It’s more inconsistent than that. Some days I’m into things, some days I’m not, and I don’t always pay enough attention to know why. So I guess… maybe a little less enjoyment at times, but I’m not sure I’d confidently say I’ve had periods where I couldn’t enjoy things at all.

I don’t know if I can say “absolutely no pleasure or interest,” especially for a full two weeks. That feels very specific, and I’d be kind of guessing if I said yes.

Like, I’ve had stretches where I’m not really excited about things, or I’ll think, “I should probably do something,” and then nothing sounds that appealing. But if I actually end up doing something, sometimes I can still get some enjoyment out of it, even if it’s not as much as I’d expect or it feels kind of muted. So it’s not like everything is completely flat all the time.

I guess the part I’m unsure about is the “at least two weeks” thing. I don’t really track it that way, so looking back it all kind of blends together. There may have been times where I was less interested for a while, but I don’t feel confident saying there was a clear two-week period where I felt no pleasure or interest at all in things I usually like. So… I’d probably say not clearly, or I’m not sure.

So yeah, a little over two years now.\n\nDoctor: Have there been any times during these two years when you felt completely back to your usual self, even for a few weeks or months?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. That’s kind of the point I keep coming back to, because before that I had normal stressful periods, but after that it just became this… ongoing thing. So for about two years and two months, I haven’t had a few weeks or months where I’d say, “Yeah, I’m back to normal.”\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep recently, like trouble falling asleep, staying asleep, or sleeping more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I haven’t really been tracking my sleep in a clear way, like writing it down or anything. Some nights feel worse than others, but I don’t know if I can honestly say there’s been a definite pattern recently, like consistently not falling asleep or waking up all night or sleeping way more.\n\nI guess I notice it more as just… I don’t wake up feeling refreshed, but I’m not sure if that’s because of my sleep itself or just because of how I’ve been feeling overall. So, yeah, I don’t know that I’ve noticed a specific sleep change recently.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, or that you’ve lost interest in activities you once found pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I know that sounds like a simple yes-or-no question, but I have a hard time separating “not enjoying things” from just being tired or distracted or going through the motions.\n\nThere are things I still do, and sometimes I can enjoy them a little once I’m already doing them, but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve clearly lost interest in everything. It’s more like I don’t always feel that same pull toward things, if that makes sense. Like I might think, “Yeah, I should do that,” but then it feels like effort to start, and by the time I do, I’m already kind of checked out.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as not enjoying things as much. I haven’t really sat down and compared it to how I used to be. I wouldn’t want to say yes too strongly if I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the past few months, have you found yourself feeling like you don’t get the same sense of pleasure or satisfaction from things, even if you used to look forward to them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that clearly, honestly. I keep trying to think of examples, like things I used to look forward to, and I can’t tell if the pleasure is actually less or if I’m just not paying attention to it the same way.\n\nLike, sometimes I’ll do something and afterward I don’t feel that satisfied, but then I also wonder if I was expecting too much from it, or if I was already kind of in my head before it even started. It’s not like I can point to one thing and say, “I used to love this and now I feel absolutely nothing.” It’s more blurry than that.\n\nSo I guess over the past few months, maybe there have been moments where things felt kind of muted, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as definitely losing pleasure or satisfaction. I haven’t really been thinking about it in those terms until you asked.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your mood typically feels first thing in the morning, before you’ve started your day or interacted with others?\n\nPatient: Usually it’s already pretty low, honestly. Like before anything has even happened, before I’ve checked my phone or talked to anyone or gotten ready for work, I’ll wake up and there’s already this heavy, gray kind of feeling there.\n\nIt’s not always like I’m thinking one specific thing right away. It’s more like the mood is already in the room before I am, if that makes sense. I’ll just be lying there and it feels like the day is already hard, even before I’ve started it. And then when I’m getting ready to go to the clinic, that feeling kind of comes with me. It doesn’t really lift just because I’m moving around or doing what I’m supposed to do.\n\nThat’s actually a big part of why I came in. It’s been going on for a long time, where the morning starts low and then it pretty much follows me through the day.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your mood has been persistently low nearly every day for the entire two years, without significant variation, regardless of what’s happening around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s been exactly the same every single day, like no variation at all, because I don’t want to make it sound more precise than I can really track. But the basic feeling, yeah, it’s been there for a long time.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. I’ll wake up and before anything has even happened, before I’ve gotten to the clinic or talked to anyone, I already feel low. It’s like this heavy gray feeling is just sitting on me. And then I get up and do what I’m supposed to do, but it doesn’t really leave. It follows me through the day, even when there isn’t some obvious thing causing it.\n\nSo maybe there are small shifts depending on the day, but overall, it feels pretty constant. Like my default mood is low, and it’s been that way long enough that I don’t really remember what a normal morning is supposed to feel like.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of years, have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight — like eating much less or more than usual, or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed anything major like that. Not in a way where I could say, “Yeah, I lost a lot of weight,” or “I’ve been eating way more than usual.” My appetite kind of depends on the day, I guess, but I haven’t been tracking it closely.\n\nSometimes I don’t feel that interested in food, but I still eat because it’s just part of the routine, or because I know I should. Other times I’ll eat normally and not really think about it. I haven’t had people telling me I look different or anything, and my clothes don’t fit noticeably differently, at least not that I’ve paid attention to.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say there’s been a significant weight change or a big appetite change that stands out to me. It’s more that I probably haven’t been paying enough attention to be totally exact.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of years, have you found yourself feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, or feeling like you’re not worth much?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I don’t walk around thinking, like, “I’m worthless” in those exact words all day or anything. And I don’t know if I’d say guilt is the main thing I notice.\n\nI do second-guess myself a lot, though, and I can be hard on myself over small things. Like if I mess something up or feel like I should’ve handled something better, I’ll replay it more than I probably need to. But I don’t know if that’s more than usual or just how I’ve always been. It’s kind of hard to separate.\n\nAs far as blaming myself for things, I guess sometimes I do, but I’m not sure it’s this huge, obvious change over the last couple years. It’s more like… if I’m already feeling low, then my mind can kind of go in that direction, like I should be doing better or I’m not where I’m supposed to be. But I don’t know if I’d describe it as constant guilt or feeling completely worthless.\n\nSo, I guess the honest answer is: I’ve noticed some self-critical thoughts, but I’m not sure how much of it counts as guilt or worthlessness in the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you've been moving more slowly than usual or, on the contrary, have been restless or fidgety more days than not?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed something obvious like people telling me I’m moving really slowly, or that I can’t sit still or anything like that.\n\nAt work, I still kind of do the things I have to do, so I’m walking around, rooming patients, getting supplies, whatever needs to be done. I don’t think anyone has said I seem physically slowed down. But at the same time, there are definitely moments where everything feels like it takes more effort, so I might be slower getting started with things. I don’t know if that counts as moving slower, though, because from the outside I might look pretty normal.\n\nAnd with restlessness, I guess I fidget sometimes, like tapping my foot or shifting around, but I’ve never really thought of it as a big symptom or something that’s happening more days than not. It’s hard for me to tell because I’m not really watching myself that way.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be: I haven’t clearly noticed either one in a major way. Maybe some sluggishness or fidgeting here and there, but nothing that stands out enough for me to say yes confidently.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, would you say there have been times when you couldn’t enjoy things that you used to genuinely like doing, even for a short while?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “couldn’t enjoy” exactly. That sounds kind of strong, like I’m totally unable to feel anything from it, and I’m not sure that’s been the case.\n\nThere have been times where things I normally like just felt… less interesting, I guess. Like I might still do them, but it feels more like I’m going through the motions than actually getting into it. But then sometimes I can still enjoy parts of things, especially if I’m already there or someone else kind of gets me involved. So it’s hard for me to separate whether I’m not enjoying it, or whether I’m just tired of the routine, or whether I’m overthinking it.\n\nI wouldn’t say there’s one clear thing where I used to love it and now I can’t stand it or get nothing from it. It’s more inconsistent than that. Some days I’m into things, some days I’m not, and I don’t always pay enough attention to know why. So I guess… maybe a little less enjoyment at times, but I’m not sure I’d confidently say I’ve had periods where I couldn’t enjoy things at all.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, have you had periods lasting at least two weeks where you felt absolutely no pleasure or interest in activities you used to genuinely enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say “absolutely no pleasure or interest,” especially for a full two weeks. That feels very specific, and I’d be kind of guessing if I said yes.\n\nLike, I’ve had stretches where I’m not really excited about things, or I’ll think, “I should probably do something,” and then nothing sounds that appealing. But if I actually end up doing something, sometimes I can still get some enjoyment out of it, even if it’s not as much as I’d expect or it feels kind of muted. So it’s not like everything is completely flat all the time.\n\nI guess the part I’m unsure about is the “at least two weeks” thing. I don’t really track it that way, so looking back it all kind of blends together. There may have been times where I was less interested for a while, but I don’t feel confident saying there was a clear two-week period where I felt no pleasure or interest at all in things I usually like. So… I’d probably say not clearly, or I’m not sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It’s more inconsistent than that. Some days I’m into things, some days I’m not, and I don’t always pay enough attention to know why. So I guess… maybe a little less enjoyment at times, but I’m not sure I’d confidently say I’ve had periods where I couldn’t enjoy things at all.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, have you had periods lasting at least two weeks where you felt absolutely no pleasure or interest in activities you used to genuinely enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say “absolutely no pleasure or interest,” especially for a full two weeks. That feels very specific, and I’d be kind of guessing if I said yes.\n\nLike, I’ve had stretches where I’m not really excited about things, or I’ll think, “I should probably do something,” and then nothing sounds that appealing. But if I actually end up doing something, sometimes I can still get some enjoyment out of it, even if it’s not as much as I’d expect or it feels kind of muted. So it’s not like everything is completely flat all the time.\n\nI guess the part I’m unsure about is the “at least two weeks” thing. I don’t really track it that way, so looking back it all kind of blends together. There may have been times where I was less interested for a while, but I don’t feel confident saying there was a clear two-week period where I felt no pleasure or interest at all in things I usually like. So… I’d probably say not clearly, or I’m not sure.\n\n[Last Question]\nOver the past couple of years, have you noticed that you've had less energy than usual or felt fatigued even after getting enough rest?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… that one I can answer more clearly. I do feel tired a lot, like way more than I think makes sense for what I’m doing.

Even if I have a day off, or I sleep late and technically should be caught up, my body still feels worn out. It’s not just like, “Oh, I’m a little sleepy.” It’s more like I wake up and I’m already kind of heavy, like I’m starting the day with my battery half-dead.

And at work, I can get through it, but by the time I’m done rooming patients and catching up on charts and running around doing the little things that come up, I feel like there’s just nothing left. Like I’ll think about doing laundry or going to shoot hoops or even messing around with electronics stuff, which is something I usually like, and it just feels like too much effort. I’ll tell myself I just need to sit down for a bit first, and then the whole evening kind of disappears.

So yeah, over the past couple years, I’d say I’ve definitely had less energy than I used to. Even resting doesn’t really reset it the way I feel like it should.

That’s kind of the point I keep coming back to, because before that I had normal stressful periods, but after that it just became this… ongoing thing. So, yeah, I don’t know that I’ve noticed a specific sleep change recently.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, or that you’ve lost interest in activities you once found pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I know that sounds like a simple yes-or-no question, but I have a hard time separating “not enjoying things” from just being tired or distracted or going through the motions.\n\nThere are things I still do, and sometimes I can enjoy them a little once I’m already doing them, but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve clearly lost interest in everything. It’s more like I don’t always feel that same pull toward things, if that makes sense. Like I might think, “Yeah, I should do that,” but then it feels like effort to start, and by the time I do, I’m already kind of checked out.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as not enjoying things as much. I haven’t really sat down and compared it to how I used to be. I wouldn’t want to say yes too strongly if I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the past few months, have you found yourself feeling like you don’t get the same sense of pleasure or satisfaction from things, even if you used to look forward to them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that clearly, honestly. I keep trying to think of examples, like things I used to look forward to, and I can’t tell if the pleasure is actually less or if I’m just not paying attention to it the same way.\n\nLike, sometimes I’ll do something and afterward I don’t feel that satisfied, but then I also wonder if I was expecting too much from it, or if I was already kind of in my head before it even started. It’s not like I can point to one thing and say, “I used to love this and now I feel absolutely nothing.” It’s more blurry than that.\n\nSo I guess over the past few months, maybe there have been moments where things felt kind of muted, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as definitely losing pleasure or satisfaction. It’s more like the mood is already in the room before I am, if that makes sense. I’ll just be lying there and it feels like the day is already hard, even before I’ve started it. It’s not like I can point to one thing and say, “I used to love this and now I feel absolutely nothing.” It’s more blurry than that.\n\nSo I guess over the past few months, maybe there have been moments where things felt kind of muted, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as definitely losing pleasure or satisfaction. I haven’t really been thinking about it in those terms until you asked.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your mood typically feels first thing in the morning, before you’ve started your day or interacted with others?\n\nPatient: Usually it’s already pretty low, honestly. Like before anything has even happened, before I’ve checked my phone or talked to anyone or gotten ready for work, I’ll wake up and there’s already this heavy, gray kind of feeling there.\n\nIt’s not always like I’m thinking one specific thing right away. It’s more like the mood is already in the room before I am, if that makes sense. I’ll just be lying there and it feels like the day is already hard, even before I’ve started it. And then when I’m getting ready to go to the clinic, that feeling kind of comes with me. It doesn’t really lift just because I’m moving around or doing what I’m supposed to do.\n\nThat’s actually a big part of why I came in. It’s been going on for a long time, where the morning starts low and then it pretty much follows me through the day.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your mood has been persistently low nearly every day for the entire two years, without significant variation, regardless of what’s happening around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s been exactly the same every single day, like no variation at all, because I don’t want to make it sound more precise than I can really track. But the basic feeling, yeah, it’s been there for a long time.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. I’ll wake up and before anything has even happened, before I’ve gotten to the clinic or talked to anyone, I already feel low. It’s like this heavy gray feeling is just sitting on me. And then I get up and do what I’m supposed to do, but it doesn’t really leave. It follows me through the day, even when there isn’t some obvious thing causing it.\n\nSo maybe there are small shifts depending on the day, but overall, it feels pretty constant. Like my default mood is low, and it’s been that way long enough that I don’t really remember what a normal morning is supposed to feel like.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of years, have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight — like eating much less or more than usual, or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed anything major like that. Not in a way where I could say, “Yeah, I lost a lot of weight,” or “I’ve been eating way more than usual.” My appetite kind of depends on the day, I guess, but I haven’t been tracking it closely.\n\nSometimes I don’t feel that interested in food, but I still eat because it’s just part of the routine, or because I know I should. Other times I’ll eat normally and not really think about it. I haven’t had people telling me I look different or anything, and my clothes don’t fit noticeably differently, at least not that I’ve paid attention to.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say there’s been a significant weight change or a big appetite change that stands out to me. It’s more that I probably haven’t been paying enough attention to be totally exact.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of years, have you found yourself feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, or feeling like you’re not worth much?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I don’t walk around thinking, like, “I’m worthless” in those exact words all day or anything. And I don’t know if I’d say guilt is the main thing I notice.\n\nI do second-guess myself a lot, though, and I can be hard on myself over small things. Like if I mess something up or feel like I should’ve handled something better, I’ll replay it more than I probably need to. But I don’t know if that’s more than usual or just how I’ve always been. It’s kind of hard to separate.\n\nAs far as blaming myself for things, I guess sometimes I do, but I’m not sure it’s this huge, obvious change over the last couple years. It’s more like… if I’m already feeling low, then my mind can kind of go in that direction, like I should be doing better or I’m not where I’m supposed to be. But I don’t know if I’d describe it as constant guilt or feeling completely worthless.\n\nSo, I guess the honest answer is: I’ve noticed some self-critical thoughts, but I’m not sure how much of it counts as guilt or worthlessness in the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you've been moving more slowly than usual or, on the contrary, have been restless or fidgety more days than not?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed something obvious like people telling me I’m moving really slowly, or that I can’t sit still or anything like that.\n\nAt work, I still kind of do the things I have to do, so I’m walking around, rooming patients, getting supplies, whatever needs to be done. I don’t think anyone has said I seem physically slowed down. But at the same time, there are definitely moments where everything feels like it takes more effort, so I might be slower getting started with things. I don’t know if that counts as moving slower, though, because from the outside I might look pretty normal.\n\nAnd with restlessness, I guess I fidget sometimes, like tapping my foot or shifting around, but I’ve never really thought of it as a big symptom or something that’s happening more days than not. It’s hard for me to tell because I’m not really watching myself that way.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be: I haven’t clearly noticed either one in a major way. Maybe some sluggishness or fidgeting here and there, but nothing that stands out enough for me to say yes confidently.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, would you say there have been times when you couldn’t enjoy things that you used to genuinely like doing, even for a short while?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “couldn’t enjoy” exactly. That sounds kind of strong, like I’m totally unable to feel anything from it, and I’m not sure that’s been the case.\n\nThere have been times where things I normally like just felt… less interesting, I guess. Like I might still do them, but it feels more like I’m going through the motions than actually getting into it. But then sometimes I can still enjoy parts of things, especially if I’m already there or someone else kind of gets me involved. So it’s hard for me to separate whether I’m not enjoying it, or whether I’m just tired of the routine, or whether I’m overthinking it.\n\nI wouldn’t say there’s one clear thing where I used to love it and now I can’t stand it or get nothing from it. It’s more inconsistent than that. Some days I’m into things, some days I’m not, and I don’t always pay enough attention to know why. So I guess… maybe a little less enjoyment at times, but I’m not sure I’d confidently say I’ve had periods where I couldn’t enjoy things at all.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, have you had periods lasting at least two weeks where you felt absolutely no pleasure or interest in activities you used to genuinely enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say “absolutely no pleasure or interest,” especially for a full two weeks. That feels very specific, and I’d be kind of guessing if I said yes.\n\nLike, I’ve had stretches where I’m not really excited about things, or I’ll think, “I should probably do something,” and then nothing sounds that appealing. But if I actually end up doing something, sometimes I can still get some enjoyment out of it, even if it’s not as much as I’d expect or it feels kind of muted. So it’s not like everything is completely flat all the time.\n\nI guess the part I’m unsure about is the “at least two weeks” thing. I don’t really track it that way, so looking back it all kind of blends together. There may have been times where I was less interested for a while, but I don’t feel confident saying there was a clear two-week period where I felt no pleasure or interest at all in things I usually like. So… I’d probably say not clearly, or I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of years, have you noticed that you've had less energy than usual or felt fatigued even after getting enough rest?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one I can answer more clearly. I do feel tired a lot, like way more than I think makes sense for what I’m doing.\n\nEven if I have a day off, or I sleep late and technically should be caught up, my body still feels worn out. It’s not just like, “Oh, I’m a little sleepy.” It’s more like I wake up and I’m already kind of heavy, like I’m starting the day with my battery half-dead.\n\nAnd at work, I can get through it, but by the time I’m done rooming patients and catching up on charts and running around doing the little things that come up, I feel like there’s just nothing left. Like I’ll think about doing laundry or going to shoot hoops or even messing around with electronics stuff, which is something I usually like, and it just feels like too much effort. I’ll tell myself I just need to sit down for a bit first, and then the whole evening kind of disappears.\n\nSo yeah, over the past couple years, I’d say I’ve definitely had less energy than I used to. Even resting doesn’t really reset it the way I feel like it should.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I can honestly say that, no. Not “most of the day, nearly every day” in a way I could point to and be sure about.

It’s more like… my interest in things comes and goes, and sometimes it’s definitely dulled. Like I’ll think about doing something I normally like and it feels like more effort than it’s worth, or I won’t feel that pull toward it the way I used to. But if I actually get into something, sometimes I still can enjoy it, at least a little. It might not feel as strong or as easy as it used to, but it’s not like the interest is completely gone all day every day.

And honestly, when I look back over two years, it’s hard to separate “I didn’t enjoy this” from “I didn’t have the energy to start it” or “I was just in a bad routine.” I don’t really track my mood or interest day by day, so saying nearly every day feels like I’d be forcing it to fit. I can say things have felt muted at times, and I don’t get excited about stuff as easily, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as a consistent, noticeable loss of pleasure for most of every day.

So, yeah, I don’t know that I’ve noticed a specific sleep change recently.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t enjoy things as much as you used to, or that you’ve lost interest in activities you once found pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I know that sounds like a simple yes-or-no question, but I have a hard time separating “not enjoying things” from just being tired or distracted or going through the motions.\n\nThere are things I still do, and sometimes I can enjoy them a little once I’m already doing them, but I don’t know if I’d say I’ve clearly lost interest in everything. It’s more like I don’t always feel that same pull toward things, if that makes sense. Like I might think, “Yeah, I should do that,” but then it feels like effort to start, and by the time I do, I’m already kind of checked out.\n\nBut I don’t know if that counts as not enjoying things as much. I haven’t really sat down and compared it to how I used to be. I wouldn’t want to say yes too strongly if I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the past few months, have you found yourself feeling like you don’t get the same sense of pleasure or satisfaction from things, even if you used to look forward to them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say that clearly, honestly. I keep trying to think of examples, like things I used to look forward to, and I can’t tell if the pleasure is actually less or if I’m just not paying attention to it the same way.\n\nLike, sometimes I’ll do something and afterward I don’t feel that satisfied, but then I also wonder if I was expecting too much from it, or if I was already kind of in my head before it even started. It’s not like I can point to one thing and say, “I used to love this and now I feel absolutely nothing.” It’s more blurry than that.\n\nSo I guess over the past few months, maybe there have been moments where things felt kind of muted, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as definitely losing pleasure or satisfaction. I haven’t really been thinking about it in those terms until you asked.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your mood typically feels first thing in the morning, before you’ve started your day or interacted with others?\n\nPatient: Usually it’s already pretty low, honestly. Like before anything has even happened, before I’ve checked my phone or talked to anyone or gotten ready for work, I’ll wake up and there’s already this heavy, gray kind of feeling there.\n\nIt’s not always like I’m thinking one specific thing right away. It’s more like the mood is already in the room before I am, if that makes sense. I’ll just be lying there and it feels like the day is already hard, even before I’ve started it. And then when I’m getting ready to go to the clinic, that feeling kind of comes with me. It doesn’t really lift just because I’m moving around or doing what I’m supposed to do.\n\nThat’s actually a big part of why I came in. It’s been going on for a long time, where the morning starts low and then it pretty much follows me through the day.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your mood has been persistently low nearly every day for the entire two years, without significant variation, regardless of what’s happening around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say it’s been exactly the same every single day, like no variation at all, because I don’t want to make it sound more precise than I can really track. But the basic feeling, yeah, it’s been there for a long time.\n\nThat’s really why I came in. I’ll wake up and before anything has even happened, before I’ve gotten to the clinic or talked to anyone, I already feel low. It’s like this heavy gray feeling is just sitting on me. And then I get up and do what I’m supposed to do, but it doesn’t really leave. It follows me through the day, even when there isn’t some obvious thing causing it.\n\nSo maybe there are small shifts depending on the day, but overall, it feels pretty constant. Like my default mood is low, and it’s been that way long enough that I don’t really remember what a normal morning is supposed to feel like.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of years, have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight — like eating much less or more than usual, or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve noticed anything major like that. Not in a way where I could say, “Yeah, I lost a lot of weight,” or “I’ve been eating way more than usual.” My appetite kind of depends on the day, I guess, but I haven’t been tracking it closely.\n\nSometimes I don’t feel that interested in food, but I still eat because it’s just part of the routine, or because I know I should. Other times I’ll eat normally and not really think about it. I haven’t had people telling me I look different or anything, and my clothes don’t fit noticeably differently, at least not that I’ve paid attention to.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say there’s been a significant weight change or a big appetite change that stands out to me. It’s more that I probably haven’t been paying enough attention to be totally exact.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of years, have you found yourself feeling guilty or blaming yourself for things more than usual, or feeling like you’re not worth much?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I don’t walk around thinking, like, “I’m worthless” in those exact words all day or anything. And I don’t know if I’d say guilt is the main thing I notice.\n\nI do second-guess myself a lot, though, and I can be hard on myself over small things. Like if I mess something up or feel like I should’ve handled something better, I’ll replay it more than I probably need to. But I don’t know if that’s more than usual or just how I’ve always been. It’s kind of hard to separate.\n\nAs far as blaming myself for things, I guess sometimes I do, but I’m not sure it’s this huge, obvious change over the last couple years. It’s more like… if I’m already feeling low, then my mind can kind of go in that direction, like I should be doing better or I’m not where I’m supposed to be. But I don’t know if I’d describe it as constant guilt or feeling completely worthless.\n\nSo, I guess the honest answer is: I’ve noticed some self-critical thoughts, but I’m not sure how much of it counts as guilt or worthlessness in the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you've been moving more slowly than usual or, on the contrary, have been restless or fidgety more days than not?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed something obvious like people telling me I’m moving really slowly, or that I can’t sit still or anything like that.\n\nAt work, I still kind of do the things I have to do, so I’m walking around, rooming patients, getting supplies, whatever needs to be done. I don’t think anyone has said I seem physically slowed down. But at the same time, there are definitely moments where everything feels like it takes more effort, so I might be slower getting started with things. I don’t know if that counts as moving slower, though, because from the outside I might look pretty normal.\n\nAnd with restlessness, I guess I fidget sometimes, like tapping my foot or shifting around, but I’ve never really thought of it as a big symptom or something that’s happening more days than not. It’s hard for me to tell because I’m not really watching myself that way.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be: I haven’t clearly noticed either one in a major way. Maybe some sluggishness or fidgeting here and there, but nothing that stands out enough for me to say yes confidently.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, would you say there have been times when you couldn’t enjoy things that you used to genuinely like doing, even for a short while?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “couldn’t enjoy” exactly. That sounds kind of strong, like I’m totally unable to feel anything from it, and I’m not sure that’s been the case.\n\nThere have been times where things I normally like just felt… less interesting, I guess. Like I might still do them, but it feels more like I’m going through the motions than actually getting into it. But then sometimes I can still enjoy parts of things, especially if I’m already there or someone else kind of gets me involved. So it’s hard for me to separate whether I’m not enjoying it, or whether I’m just tired of the routine, or whether I’m overthinking it.\n\nI wouldn’t say there’s one clear thing where I used to love it and now I can’t stand it or get nothing from it. It’s more inconsistent than that. Some days I’m into things, some days I’m not, and I don’t always pay enough attention to know why. So I guess… maybe a little less enjoyment at times, but I’m not sure I’d confidently say I’ve had periods where I couldn’t enjoy things at all.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, have you had periods lasting at least two weeks where you felt absolutely no pleasure or interest in activities you used to genuinely enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can say “absolutely no pleasure or interest,” especially for a full two weeks. That feels very specific, and I’d be kind of guessing if I said yes.\n\nLike, I’ve had stretches where I’m not really excited about things, or I’ll think, “I should probably do something,” and then nothing sounds that appealing. But if I actually end up doing something, sometimes I can still get some enjoyment out of it, even if it’s not as much as I’d expect or it feels kind of muted. So it’s not like everything is completely flat all the time.\n\nI guess the part I’m unsure about is the “at least two weeks” thing. I don’t really track it that way, so looking back it all kind of blends together. There may have been times where I was less interested for a while, but I don’t feel confident saying there was a clear two-week period where I felt no pleasure or interest at all in things I usually like. So… I’d probably say not clearly, or I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Over the past couple of years, have you noticed that you've had less energy than usual or felt fatigued even after getting enough rest?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that one I can answer more clearly. I do feel tired a lot, like way more than I think makes sense for what I’m doing.\n\nEven if I have a day off, or I sleep late and technically should be caught up, my body still feels worn out. It’s not just like, “Oh, I’m a little sleepy.” It’s more like I wake up and I’m already kind of heavy, like I’m starting the day with my battery half-dead.\n\nAnd at work, I can get through it, but by the time I’m done rooming patients and catching up on charts and running around doing the little things that come up, I feel like there’s just nothing left. Like I’ll think about doing laundry or going to shoot hoops or even messing around with electronics stuff, which is something I usually like, and it just feels like too much effort. I’ll tell myself I just need to sit down for a bit first, and then the whole evening kind of disappears.\n\nSo yeah, over the past couple years, I’d say I’ve definitely had less energy than I used to. Even resting doesn’t really reset it the way I feel like it should.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that, for most of the day, nearly every day over the past two years, you’ve felt a noticeable loss of pleasure or interest in things you used to enjoy, even if it wasn’t always the same intensity every single day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can honestly say that, no. Not “most of the day, nearly every day” in a way I could point to and be sure about.\n\nIt’s more like… my interest in things comes and goes, and sometimes it’s definitely dulled. Like I’ll think about doing something I normally like and it feels like more effort than it’s worth, or I won’t feel that pull toward it the way I used to. But if I actually get into something, sometimes I still can enjoy it, at least a little. It might not feel as strong or as easy as it used to, but it’s not like the interest is completely gone all day every day.\n\nAnd honestly, when I look back over two years, it’s hard to separate “I didn’t enjoy this” from “I didn’t have the energy to start it” or “I was just in a bad routine.” I don’t really track my mood or interest day by day, so saying nearly every day feels like I’d be forcing it to fit. I can say things have felt muted at times, and I don’t get excited about stuff as easily, but I’m not sure I’d describe it as a consistent, noticeable loss of pleasure for most of every day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Yeah… I think that’s actually a big part of why I came in.

It’s like I wake up already low, before anything has even happened. Like I open my eyes and there’s this heavy, gray feeling on me, and then I have to get up and go to the clinic and act normal and do what I’m supposed to do. But it’s already there before I even get there.

And then through the day, even if something good happens, it doesn’t really clear it out. Maybe I can laugh for a second, or I can talk to someone I care about and it helps a little in the moment, but it’s like the low feeling is still running in the background. It doesn’t fully lift. It’s not always dramatic, like I’m visibly upset every second, but it’s just… there. Like a weight I’m carrying around.

So yeah, I’d say even around good things or good people, my mood can shift a little on the surface, but underneath it still feels pretty low most of the time.
